Unconditional uniqueness for the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation by normal form approach
Nobu Kishimoto

Abstract
We prove uniqueness of solutions to the Cauchy problem for the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation in L t ∞ ​ H x 1 / 2 L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1/2}_{x} . Our proof is based on the method of normal form reduction (NFR), which has been employed to obtain the uniqueness in C t ​ H x s C_{t}H^{s}_{x} , s > 1 / 2 s>1/2 . To overcome logarithmic divergences at the H 1 / 2 H^{1/2} regularity, we exploit the B ∞ , 1 0 + B^{0+}_{\infty,1} control of solutions provided by a refined Strichartz estimate. Our NFR argument consists of two stages: we first use NFR finitely many times to derive an intermediate equation in which the main cubic nonlinearity is restricted to a certain type of frequency interaction; we then apply the infinite NFR scheme to the intermediate equation. Moreover, we modify the usual NFR argument relying on continuity in time of solutions so that the uniqueness in the class L t ∞ ​ H x 1 / 2 L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1/2}_{x} can be obtained directly.



1
Introduction

We consider uniqueness of solutions to the Cauchy problem for the one-dimensional cubic derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation (DNLS)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u

\displaystyle=i\lambda\partial_{x}\big(|u|^{2}u\big),

\displaystyle t\in(-T,T),\quad x\in\mathcal{M},

(1.1)

\displaystyle u(0,x)

\displaystyle=u_{0}(x),

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{M},

(1.2)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is a non-zero real constant, the spatial domain
\mathcal{M}
is either the real line
\mathbb{R}
or the torus
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, and the initial datum
u_{0}
is given in
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.

Well-posedness of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) has been extensively studied; we refer the reader to
[
5
,
4
]
for a detailed description of previous works.
It has been proved to be well-posed in the scaling-critical space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
in the non-periodic case (
[
4
]
) and in
H^{\frac{1}{6}}(\mathbb{T})
in the periodic case (
[
5
]
).
Currently, all the known well-posedness results below
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
rely on the complete integrability of (
1.1
).
Note that the (local-in-time) well-posedness in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
was obtained in
[
14
,
6
]
by a gauge transformation and a fixed-point argument in Fourier restriction norm spaces (Bourgain spaces), without utilizing complete integrability.

A standard definition of well-posedness in a space
X
of initial data requires existence of a solution in
C_{t}X
, its uniqueness, and its continuous dependence on initial data in the topology of
X
.
Concerning the uniqueness, in many cases it is initially obtained in a space smaller than
C_{t}X
or in some weaker sense (e.g., the unique limit of regular solutions starting from regularized initial data), and then the uniqueness in the entire class
C_{t}X
is considered as a separate problem.
The latter property is called
unconditional uniqueness
, and the Cauchy problem is said to be unconditionally well-posed if it is well-posed and the uniqueness holds unconditionally (cf.
[
7
]
).
Unconditional uniqueness is a concept that is independent of how solutions are constructed.

Regarding the well-posedness results on DNLS (
1.1
), those below
H^{\frac{3}{2}}
were (originally) conditional ones.
For instance, the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
results in
[
14
,
6
]
provide uniqueness of solutions only in a suitable Bourgain space, which is smaller than
C_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Later, the unconditional uniqueness in
H^{1}
was proved in
[
15
]
and that in
H^{s}
with
s>1/2
was obtained in
[
13
,
8
]
for both
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
.
On the other hand, one can argue for the uniqueness in
C_{t}H^{s}
only if the nonlinearity is well-defined for a general function in
C_{t}H^{s}
.
This gives the lower bound of the Sobolev exponent
s\geq 1/6
for unconditional uniqueness for (
1.1
) in view of the 1D Sobolev embedding
H^{\frac{1}{6}}\subset L^{3}
.
In this article, we aim to fill the gap
1/2\geq s\geq 1/6
, and at the first onset we consider the endpoint regularity
s=1/2
, which is likely to be solved without utilizing complete integrability.

For
s\geq 1/6
, we can show (see Appendix
A
) that any function
u\in L^{\infty}(-T,T;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
solving (
1.1
) in the sense of distributions belongs to
C([-T,T];H^{s-\varepsilon}(\mathcal{M}))\cap C_{w}([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
1
1

1

More precisely,
u
coincides for
a.e.

t\in(-T,T)
with a unique function which belongs to
C([-T,T];H^{s-\varepsilon}(\mathcal{M}))\cap C_{w}([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
; we will always identify
u
with this continuous representative.
Here,
C_{w}
means the class of weakly continuous functions.

In particular, the initial value
u(0)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
is well-defined.
Then, we say
u\in L^{\infty}(-T,T;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
is a solution to the initial value problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with the initial datum
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
if
u
is a solution of (
1.1
) on
(-T,T)\times\mathcal{M}
in the sense of distributions and
u
satisfies the initial condition (
1.2
) in the above sense.
Now, we state the main theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

For both
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
, the solution to the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) is unique in
L^{\infty}(-T,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{M}))
.

Remark 1.2
.

(i) It may not seem to be very meaningful to focus on the particular regularity
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
for DNLS.
Indeed, we are motivated by the same problem for the modified Benjamin-Ono equation (mBO).
On one hand, many techniques can be applied to these two equations in a similar manner; e.g., the gauge transformation, multilinear estimates in Fourier restriction norms, the normal form reduction, and so on.
On the other hand, mBO is not known to be completely integrable.
Moreover, the space
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
has the special importance for mBO, as it is the energy space.
In particular, similarly to the case of the Benjamin-Ono equation, we expect that the unconditional uniqueness in the energy space could be used to justify the inviscid limit (i.e., considering the perturbed equation with the viscosity term
\varepsilon\partial_{x}^{2}u
and taking the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
; see
[
10
]
).
Since both the nonlinear interactions and the gauge transformation are much simpler for DNLS than for mBO, we intend to study DNLS first.

(ii) We aim to prove the uniqueness in a wider class
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
rather than in
C_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
In fact, this stronger uniqueness assertion is required in the application to the inviscid limit problem mentioned above.
Recall that the difference between
C_{t}H^{s}
and
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}
is not relevant when
s>1/2
, because a solution in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}
necessarily belongs to
C_{t}H^{s^{\prime}}
for any
s^{\prime}\in(1/2,s)
as seen above and we can apply the uniqueness in
C_{t}H^{s^{\prime}}
.
However, at the limiting regularity the uniqueness in
C_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
does not yield that in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
by the same argument, and we have to prove the latter directly.

Here, we describe our strategy of the proof.
The unconditional uniqueness in
H^{s}
for
s>1/2
was proved (
[
13
,
8
]
) by employing the gauge transformation used for local well-posedness in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
(
[
14
,
6
]
) and the
normal form reduction
argument (NFR, for short).
The latter method is based on simple integration-by-parts operations and it has been frequently used for the unconditional uniqueness problem.
It was first used for the periodic KdV equation in
[
1
]
, and later for the periodic 1D cubic NLS in
[
3
]
where the infinite iteration scheme of NFR was first introduced, and then developed also for the non-periodic problem since
[
9
]
; see
[
8
]
for a survey of the method.
In particular, some problems (such as the KdV equation, the modified KdV equation on
\mathbb{T}
and the Benjamin-Ono equation) needed only finite-time applications of NFR, while others (such as NLS, DNLS, the modified KdV on
\mathbb{R}
and the modified Benjamin-Ono equation) required infinite iteration scheme.
To prove Theorem
1.1
, we first apply the same gauge transformation as before and consider the transformed equation in which the nonlinearity has become essentially the cubic term
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
.
For the case
s>1/2
in
[
13
,
8
]
, the infinite NFR scheme was successfully applied to this equation.
However, it seems difficult to adapt the same argument to the case
s=1/2
due to several reasons.

First of all, the nonlinear term
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
is not well-defined in the framework of distributions for a general function
v
in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
(while it is well-defined for
v\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}
,
s>1/2
, by the Sobolev inequalities).
This means that one cannot argue uniqueness of solutions in the entire class
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
(or
C_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
) for the transformed equation.
The key ingredient to bypass this obstacle at
s=1/2
is the so-called
refined Strichartz estimate
, which was recently used in
[
12
]
to improve the unconditional uniqueness result for the Benjamin-Ono equation via the NFR method.
We begin with an arbitrary solution
u\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
to (
1.1
).
Then,
u
satisfies the associated integral equation

u(t)=e^{i(t-t_{0})\partial_{x}^{2}}u(t_{0})+\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{i(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{x}^{2}}\partial_{x}\big(|u(t^{\prime})|^{2}u(t^{\prime})\big)\,dt^{\prime},\quad t,t_{0}\in[-T,T].

The refined Strichartz estimate (Proposition
2.1
below) is derived from this integral representation and the standard Strichartz estimate (for linear solutions) on short intervals.
It asserts that a solution
u
has better
L^{p}
property in
x
(after averaging in
t
) than that predicted by the Sobolev embedding.
For instance, we will show
u\in L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}
for any solution
u\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
, despite that
H^{\frac{1}{2}}\not\subset L^{\infty}
.
Since the gauge transform
v
of
u
satisfies
|v|=|u|
, it also belongs to
L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}
in addition to
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Hence, it suffices to prove the uniqueness for the transformed equation in the smaller class
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}
.
We can use this additional property and the Moser estimate

\|fg\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}

to make sense of the nonlinearity
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
(see Lemma
2.3
below).

Another (in fact, much more serious) difficulty is to overcome various logarithmic divergences arising in nonlinear estimates at the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
level.
Now, the refined Strichartz estimate actually shows that
u\in L^{4}_{t}B^{\varepsilon}_{\infty,1}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
.
We thus have an extra
\varepsilon
regularity from the scaling viewpoint, and this is the key to control nonlinear terms in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
The cost of using
L^{\infty}_{x}
-type norms is that it is not allowed to freely take absolute values on the Fourier side.
Indeed, the
L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}
-Strichartz estimates do not hold for
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\big|\mathcal{F}_{x}[e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}]\big|\big]
unless
q=2
, and similarly the refined Strichartz estimate fails for
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[|\mathcal{F}_{x}u|]
.
Although this point was not so serious in the study of the Benjamin-Ono equation
[
12
]
, but this is not the case for our problem, and we need to prepare Coifman-Meyer type multilinear estimates (see Subsection
2.4
for more details).

We have seen that whether the NFR procedure finishes in finite times or continues infinitely depends on the problem.
In general, finiteness of the iteration allows us to make more precise evaluation and recover more derivatives at each step.
However, especially when the “non-resonance” property of the nonlinear interaction is not strong, a finite number of applications are not enough to recover sufficient derivatives and then an infinite iteration is required.
In this case, one will be forced to make somewhat rough evaluation at each step in order to deal with the increasingly complicated resonance structures (phase functions) in a unified manner.
This is also one of the reasons why the infinite NFR scheme does not work as well when
s=1/2
.
To address this issue, we use the finite and infinite iteration schemes for different parts of the nonlinearity
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
.
More specifically, we use the infinite iteration only to the “high
\times
high
\times
high
\to
high” frequency interaction, for which the resonant function
2(\xi_{1}+\xi_{3})(\xi_{2}+\xi_{3})
is possible to become much smaller than each frequency
|\xi_{j}|
, while for the other types of interactions we apply NFR up to twice and make a closer investigation of each interaction at each step.
We also note that the
B^{\varepsilon}_{\infty,1}
control of the solution will be used only in the finite iteration part, since logarithmic divergence does not occur in the “high
\times
high
\times
high
\to
high” interaction case.
Our hybrid NFR procedure will be described in more detail in Section
3
.

Finally, as mentioned in Remark
1.2
(ii), we need to prove the uniqueness in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
directly rather than in
C_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
The only issue is how to justify various formal computations for a general solution, and the existing results based on NFR are all assuming continuity in
t
for this purpose.
We will confirm that the argument can be modified so that the computations are justified under the weaker assumption
u\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
(see Subsections
4.5
,
5.3
).

This article is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we transform the problem by the gauge transformation and prepare several lemmas (refined Strichartz estimates and Coifman-Meyer type estimates) used in our NFR argument.
In Section
3
, we classify frequency interactions of the main cubic nonlinear term and outline our two-stage NFR procedure.
The detailed analysis is provided in the following two sections.
Section
4
describes the first stage, where we apply the finite NFR iteration scheme to the most part of the nonlinearity.
As a conclusion of this stage, we obtain the intermediate equation (see (
4.16
) and (
4.21
)) in which the cubic interaction is restricted to the “high
\times
high
\times
high
\to
high” case and the other nonlinear parts have been transformed into the form that admits the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
control.
The second stage consists of applying the infinite NFR scheme to the intermediate equation, and this is performed in Section
5
.
Finally, a proof of Theorem
1.1
is given in Subsection
5.4
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

•

We write
X\lesssim Y
for the estimate
X\leq CY
with a constant
C>0
.

X\sim Y
means
X\lesssim Y\lesssim X
.
By
X\gg Y
we mean
X\geq CY
with a suitably large constant
C>1
.
For a real number
a
, we write
a+
,
a-
to denote real numbers
a+\varepsilon
,
a-\varepsilon
with a suitably small
\varepsilon>0
.

•

We will use the abbreviations
L^{p}_{T}X:=L^{p}_{t}(-T,T;X)
,
C_{T}X:=C_{t}([-T,T];X)
for
T>0
and a space
X
of functions in
x
.

•

The Fourier and the inverse Fourier transforms (in the spatial variable) are denoted by
\mathcal{F}u
or
\hat{u}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}u
or
\check{u}
, respectively.
We use the definition

\hat{u}(\xi):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}u(x)e^{-i\xi x}\,dx,\qquad\xi\in\hat{\mathcal{M}}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{R}&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}),\\
&\mathbb{Z}&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}),\end{aligned}\right.

so that

u(x)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\hat{u}](x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\hat{\mathcal{M}}}\hat{u}(\xi)e^{i\xi x}\,d\xi:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{u}(\xi)e^{i\xi x}\,d\xi&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}),\\
&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{u}(\xi)e^{i\xi x}&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}).\end{aligned}\right.

The linear Schrödinger propagator is denoted by

S(t):=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}e^{-it\xi^{2}}\mathcal{F}.

In the periodic setting, we also use the following projection operators:

P_{c}u:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\hat{u}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,dx,\qquad P_{\neq c}u:=u-P_{c}u=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\hat{u}(\xi)e^{i\xi x}.

•

The characteristic function of a set
\Omega
is denoted by
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}
. We often indicate only the conditions determining
\Omega
.

•

Related to the Littlewood-Paley decomposition, we denote dyadic integers
1,2,4,8,\dots
by capital letters
N,L,K,\dots
.
We use only the inhomogeneous decomposition.

•

Let
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function satisfying
\mathbf{1}_{[-5/4,5/4]}\leq\eta\leq\mathbf{1}_{[-8/5,8/5]}
.
2
2

2

We impose this support condition rather than
\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}\leq\eta\leq\mathbf{1}_{[-2,2]}
just in order to define the functions
\tilde{\psi}_{N}
below.
The specific numbers
\frac{5}{4}
,
\frac{8}{5}
do not play any role.

Define the Littlewood-Paley decomposition
\{P_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
by
P_{N}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\psi_{N}\mathcal{F}
, where

\psi_{N}(\xi):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta(\frac{|\xi|}{N})-\eta(\frac{2|\xi|}{N})&\quad&(N\geq 2),\\
&\eta(|\xi|)&&(N=1).\end{aligned}\right.

We see that the function
\psi_{N}
(smooth, even, defined on
\mathbb{R}
) is supported in
I_{N}
, where

I_{N}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\{\xi:N/2\leq|\xi|\leq 2N\}&\quad&(N\geq 2),\\
&\{\xi:|\xi|\leq 2\}&&(N=1).\end{aligned}\right.

We sometimes use
\{\tilde{\psi}_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
, smooth functions satisfying
\textbf{1}_{\mathrm{supp}\,(\psi_{N})}\leq\tilde{\psi}_{N}\leq\mathbf{1}_{I_{N}}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{N}(\xi)=\tilde{\psi}_{2}(\xi/(N/2))
(
N\geq 2
).

•

The (inhomogeneous) Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})=B^{s}_{2,2}(\mathcal{M})
and Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathcal{M})
are defined by

\|u\|_{H^{s}}:=\|\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\hat{u}(\xi)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\sim\|u\|_{B^{s}_{2,2}},\quad\|u\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}:=\big\|N^{s}\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\big\|_{\ell^{q}_{N}},

where
\langle\xi\rangle:=(1+\xi^{2})^{1/2}
.
We will estimate the solution in the space
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
.
For short, we write its norm as
\|\cdot\|_{S_{T}}
:

\|w\|_{S_{T}}:=\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|w\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}.

•

\hat{H}^{s}:=\langle\cdot\rangle^{-s}L^{2}
denotes the weighted
L^{2}
space equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\hat{H}^{s}}:=\|\langle\xi\rangle^{s}f(\xi)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.

•

We will use the abbreviation
\xi_{ij\dots}
for
\xi_{i}+\xi_{j}+\dots
.
For instance,
\xi_{13}:=\xi_{1}+\xi_{3}
and
\eta_{123}:=\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\eta_{3}
.

2.2
Refined Strichartz estimates

Let
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{M})
satisfy the equation (
1.1
) in the sense of distributions.
We can verify (see Appendix
A
) that
u\in C_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}(\mathcal{M})\cap C_{w,T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{M})
and
u
satisfies the integral equation

\displaystyle u(t)=S(t-t_{0})u(t_{0})+\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{t}S(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{x}\big(|u(t^{\prime})|^{2}u(t^{\prime})\big)\,dt^{\prime}

(2.1)

\displaystyle\text{in $H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}(\mathcal{M})$},\qquad\forall t,t_{0}\in[-T,T].

The following a priori estimate, which is called a refined Strichartz estimate, holds equally for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
T>0
, and suppose that
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
is a solution of (
1.1
).
Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\Big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}+}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}N^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\Big)\Big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\Big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad(N\geq 1).

(2.2)

In particular,
u\in L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}
for
s<\frac{1}{4}
.
Moreover, for
T\in(0,1]
and
s\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
,

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}\lesssim\Big(T^{\frac{1}{4}-s-}+T^{\frac{1}{2}-s-}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\Big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(2.3)

Proof.

Proof of (
2.2
):

If
T\geq N^{-1}
, we divide the time interval
[-T,T]
into
\sim TN
sub-intervals
I_{j}:=[t_{j},t_{j+1}]
of size
\sim N^{-1}
.
In the periodic setting, it is known that the same Strichartz estimates as those on
\mathbb{R}
hold for short time length
\lesssim N^{-1}
if the frequency is restricted to
\{|\xi|\leq N\}
; in particular, it holds that

\displaystyle\|S(t-t_{j})P_{N}u(t_{j})\|_{L^{4}(I_{j};L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|P_{N}u(t_{j})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})},

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{t_{j}}^{t}S(t-t^{\prime})P_{N}F(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\Big\|_{L^{4}(I_{j};L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|P_{N}F\|_{L^{1}(I_{j};L^{2}(\mathcal{M}))}

for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{T}
.
Using (
2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j}\Big(\|P_{N}u(t_{j})\|_{L^{2}}^{4}+\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{1}(I_{j};L^{2})}^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim TN\big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big)^{4}+\sum_{j}\Big(\big(N^{-1}\big)^{\frac{3}{4}}\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{4}(I_{j};L^{2})}\Big)^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim TN^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}+N^{-3}\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{2}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim TN^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}+TN^{-1+}\|\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}^{4}.

By the Sobolev inequalities, we have

\|\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{3}.

Combining these estimates, we have

\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}N^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

If
T\leq N^{-1}
, we apply the above short-time Strichartz estimates on the whole interval
[-T,T]
and Bernstein’s inequalities to obtain

\displaystyle\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|P_{N}u(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{1}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|u(0)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+TN^{\frac{1}{2}+}\|\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}+}\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

We have thus proved (
2.2
).

Proof of (
2.3
):

From (
2.2
), we have

\|P_{>M}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\Big(M^{-\frac{1}{2}+}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}M^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\Big)\Big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\Big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

On the other hand, from Hölder and Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\|P_{\leq M}u\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\langle\log M\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

For
0<T\leq 1
, we take
M\sim T^{-1}
and obtain (
2.3
) with
s=0
.
The case
s\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
is proved in a similar way.
∎

2.3
Gauge transformation

Next, we recall the gauge transformation used in
[
14
,
6
]
.

Definition 2.1

(Gauge transformation)
.

The spatial antiderivative
\partial_{x}^{-1}
is defined by

\partial_{x}^{-1}f(x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{-\infty}^{x}f(y)\,dy\qquad\text{(for $f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})$, if $\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}$)},\\
&\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\int_{z}^{x}f(y)\,dy\Big)\,dz=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[(i\xi)^{-1}\hat{f}(\xi)\big](x)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{(for $f$ with $P_{c}f=0$, if $\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}$)}.\end{aligned}\right.

For
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, we define

v(t):=e^{-i\lambda J(u(t))}u(t),\qquad J(u):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{x}^{-1}(|u|^{2})&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}),\\
&\partial_{x}^{-1}P_{\neq c}(|u|^{2})&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}),\end{aligned}\right.

and

w(t,x):=v\Big(t,x-2\lambda\int_{0}^{t}P_{c}(|v(t^{\prime})|^{2})\,dt^{\prime}\Big)\qquad(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}).

Since
|v(t,x)|=|u(t,x)|
and
P_{c}(|w(t)|^{2})=P_{c}(|v(t)|^{2})
, the mappings
u\mapsto v
,
v\mapsto w
are invertible and the inverse mappings are given by

\displaystyle v(t,x)\quad

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad u(t,x):=e^{i\lambda J(v(t))}v(t,x),

\displaystyle w(t,x)\quad

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad v(t,x):=w\Big(t,x+2\lambda\int_{0}^{t}P_{c}(|w(t^{\prime})|^{2})\,dt^{\prime}\Big).

In the proof of unconditional uniqueness, we use the bijectivity on
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
of the gauge transformation:
u\mapsto v
for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
u\mapsto w
for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
.
Note that the mapping
u\mapsto v
is locally bi-Lipschitz on
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}
for each
s\geq 0
.
(For the periodic case, see
[
6
, Lemma 2.3]
.
For the non-periodic case, a similar argument with the fractional Leibniz rule estimates would work.
A proof for
0\leq s\leq 1/2
will be essentially given in the proof of Lemma
2.5
(i) and Lemma
2.6
(i) below.)

If
u
is a solution to (
1.1
), the equation for its gauge transform is
formally
given as follows.
In the non-periodic case,
v
solves

\partial_{t}v=i\partial_{x}^{2}v-\lambda v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}+i\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}|v|^{4}v.

(2.4)

In the periodic case,
v
and
w
solve

\displaystyle\,\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v&=i\partial_{x}^{2}v-\lambda\Big(v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}-2P_{c}(v\partial_{x}\bar{v})v\Big)+2\lambda P_{c}(|v|^{2})\partial_{x}v\\
&\quad+i\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|v|^{4})v-P_{c}(|v|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|v|^{2})v\Big),\end{aligned}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}w&=i\partial_{x}^{2}w-\lambda\Big(w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}-2P_{c}(w\partial_{x}\bar{w})w\Big)\\
&\quad+i\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|w|^{4})w-P_{c}(|w|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|w|^{2})w\Big).\end{aligned}

(2.6)

An important step in the problem of unconditional uniqueness is to verify these equations for a general (distributional) solution
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
of (
1.1
), which we address in the next proposition.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
be a distributional solution of (
1.1
), and let
v,w
be defined as in Definition
2.1
.
Then,
u,v,w\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}
, and
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
,
w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}
are well-defined as distributions in
L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.
Moreover,
v
and
w
are solutions of (
2.4
) or (
2.5
), and respectively (
2.6
), on
(-T,T)\times\mathcal{M}
in the sense of distributions.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.1
we have
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}
, and hence, by definition,
v
,
w
belong to
L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}
.
The fact that
v,w\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
will be shown later.
Then, the nonlinear terms
v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}
,
w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}
can be defined as distributions in
L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
, using the next lemma:

Lemma 2.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\|f_{1}f_{2}\partial_{x}\bar{f}_{3}\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}\lesssim\Big(

\displaystyle\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big)\|f_{3}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

In particular, if
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}
and
f_{3}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then
f_{1}f_{2}\partial_{x}\bar{f}_{3}\in L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.

Proof.

This follows from the Sobolev inequality

\|fg\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}}

and the Moser estimate

\|fg\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}.\qed

To justify the equation (
2.4
) or (
2.5
) for
v
, we consider smooth approximation
v_{N}
defined by

v_{N}:=e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}u_{N},\qquad u_{N}:=P_{\leq N}u.

(2.7)

First, we prove the following:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
be a distributional solution of (
1.1
) on
(-T,T)
, and define
u_{N}:=P_{\leq N}u
.
Then, the following holds.

\mathrm{(i)}

u\in L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
and
u_{N}\to u
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\to 0
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

L^{2}
conservation law:
\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}}=\|u(0)\|_{L^{2}}
for
t\in[-T,T]
.
3
3

3

Recall that (after modifying on a set of measure zero) a distributional solution
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
to (
1.1
) has the regularity
C([-T,T];H^{\frac{1}{2}-})
.

Proof.

(i) Note that
P_{\leq N}f\to f
in
H^{s}
for any
f\in H^{s}
.
Then,
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
and the dominated convergence theorem imply the convergence in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
The convergence in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
follows from the fact that
u\in C_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
.
By interpolation between (
2.2
) and

\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}},

we have

\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{T}N^{-\min\{\frac{1}{p},\frac{1}{4}\}+}\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(2.8)

Therefore, we see
u\in L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
and that

\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{p,T,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}}N^{-\min\{\frac{1}{p},\frac{1}{4}\}+}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty).

(ii) It suffices to show the convergence of
P_{>N}(|u|^{2}u)
and
|u|^{2}u-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}
to
0
.
By
u\in C_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
and the Sobolev inequality
\|fg\|_{H^{s_{1}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s_{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{s_{2}}}
for
0<s_{1}<\frac{1}{2}
with
s_{2}:=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+\frac{1}{2})
, we see
|u|^{2}u\in C_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
.
Since the Moser estimate with
u\in L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
implies
|u|^{2}u\in L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
, the convergence of
P_{>N}(|u|^{2}u)
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
follows similarly to (i).
From the support property of
\psi_{N}
we see that
P_{>N}(|P_{\leq N/4}u|^{2}P_{\leq N/4}u)=0
, from which we have

\|P_{>N}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{3p}_{T}L^{\infty}}^{2}\|P_{>N/4}u\|_{L^{3p}_{T}L^{\infty}}.

The convergence in
L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
follows from (i).
For
|u|^{2}u-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}
, the convergence in these norms can be shown by using Sobolev, Moser and Hölder and the convergence in (i).

(iii) Since
u_{N}
is a solution (in the classical sense) of

\partial_{t}u_{N}=i\partial_{x}^{2}u_{N}+\lambda\partial_{x}P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u),

a direct calculation shows that for any
N\geq 1

\|u_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|u_{N}(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda\Re\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{u}_{N}\partial_{x}\Big(P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\Big)\,dx\,dt^{\prime},\quad t\in[-T,T].

The last integral is bounded by
2|\lambda|\|u_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\|_{L^{1}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}
.
Then, the
L^{2}
conservation is verified by taking the limit
N\to\infty
with the convergences in (i), (ii).
∎

From here, we consider the periodic and non-periodic cases separately.

\ll\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}\gg

After a long calculation (see
[
8
, Section 5.1]
for details), we see that
v_{N}
satisfies

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-i\partial_{x}^{2})v_{N}

\displaystyle=-\lambda\Big(v_{N}^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}_{N}-2P_{c}(v_{N}\partial_{x}\bar{v}_{N})v_{N}\Big)+2\lambda P_{c}(|v_{N}|^{2})\partial_{x}v_{N}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\quad+i\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|v_{N}|^{4})v_{N}-P_{c}(|v_{N}|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|v_{N}|^{2})v_{N}\Big)

(2.10)

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}\partial_{x}\Big(P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\Big)

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad-2i\lambda^{2}v_{N}\partial_{x}^{-1}P_{\neq c}\Re\Big[\bar{u}_{N}\partial_{x}\Big(P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\Big)\Big].

(2.12)

We note that the equation is satisfied in the classical sense, because
u_{N},v_{N}\in C^{1}_{T}H^{\infty}
and
P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)\in C_{T}H^{\infty}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
and
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
be a distributional solution of (
1.1
) on
(-T,T)
, and define
v,u_{N},v_{N}
as in Definition
2.1
and in (
2.7
).
Then, the following holds.

\mathrm{(i)}

v\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
and
v_{N}\to v
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

\eqref{term1}\to-\lambda(v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}-2P_{c}(v\partial_{x}\bar{v})v)+2\lambda P_{c}(|v|^{2})\partial_{x}v
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

\eqref{term2}\to i\lambda^{2}\big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|v|^{4})v-P_{c}(|v|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|v|^{2})v\big)
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(iv)}

\eqref{term3}\to 0
in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.

\mathrm{(v)}

\eqref{term4}\to 0
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

Consequently,
v
is a distributional solution of (
2.5
) on
(-T,T)
.

Proof.

(i) We have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|e^{-i\lambda J(f)}\|_{H^{1}}&\lesssim\|e^{-i\lambda J(f)}\|_{L^{2}}+\|P_{\neq c}(|f|^{2})e^{-i\lambda J(f)}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim 1+\|f\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\lesssim 1+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}^{2},\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|e^{-i\lambda J(f)}-e^{-i\lambda J(g)}\|_{H^{1}}&\lesssim\|J(f)-J(g)\|_{L^{2}}+\|P_{\neq c}(|f|^{2}-|g|^{2})\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\|P_{\neq c}(|g|^{2})[J(f)-J(g)]\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim(\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}+\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}})(1+\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}^{2})\|f-g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}.\end{aligned}

From the Sobolev inequality
\|fg\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}
and Lemma
2.4
(i), we see that

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\Big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}}}^{2}\Big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|v_{N}-v\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}&\lesssim\|e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}-e^{-i\lambda J(u)}\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{1}}\|u_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|e^{-i\lambda J(u)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{1}}\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&\lesssim_{\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty).\end{aligned}

Also, from Lemma
2.4
(i) we see
v\in L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
and

\displaystyle\|v_{N}-v\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|P_{\neq c}(|u_{N}|^{2}-|u|^{2})\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{1}}\|u_{N}\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}+\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}+\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty).

(ii) Lemma
2.3
and (i) imply the convergence of the first term in
L^{p}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.
Since

|P_{c}(v_{1}\partial_{x}\bar{v}_{2})|+|P_{c}(v_{1}\bar{v}_{2})|\lesssim\|v_{1}\|_{H^{1/2}}\|v_{2}\|_{H^{1/2}},

by using (i) we obtain the convergence of the last two terms in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
and in
L^{p}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, respectively.

(iii) This is straightforward, using (i) and the Sobolev inequality
\|fg\|_{H^{s_{1}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s_{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}
for
0<s_{1}<s_{2}<\frac{1}{2}
.

(iv) By the Sobolev inequality
\|fg\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}}\|g\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}
, the claim is reduced to

\|P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty),

which has been shown in Lemma
2.4
(ii).

(v) By the Sobolev inequalities

\|fg\|_{H^{s_{1}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{s_{2}}}\quad(0<s_{1}<s_{2}<1/2),\qquad\|fg\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}},

it suffices to show that

\|P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty),

which has been shown in Lemma
2.4
(ii).
∎

We have shown that
v
is a solution of (
2.5
) in the sense of distributions.
Noticing that
P_{c}(|v(t)|^{2})
is a constant by Lemma
2.4
(iii), it is easy to see that
w\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
and it satisfies the equation (
2.6
) in the sense of distributions.

\ll\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\gg

A direct calculation shows that
v_{N}
is a (classical) solution of

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-i\partial_{x}^{2})v_{N}

\displaystyle=-\lambda v_{N}^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}_{N}+i\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}|v_{N}|^{4}v_{N}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}\partial_{x}\Big(P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\Big)

(2.14)

\displaystyle\quad-2i\lambda^{2}v_{N}\Re\int_{-\infty}^{x}\bar{u}_{N}\partial_{x}\Big(P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}\Big)\,dy.

(2.15)

It suffices to prove the following lemma:

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
be a distributional solution of (
1.1
) on
(-T,T)
, and define
v,u_{N},v_{N}
as in Definition
2.1
and in (
2.7
).
Then, the following holds.

\mathrm{(i)}

v\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
and
v_{N}\to v
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

\eqref{term1R}\to-\lambda v^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{v}+i\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}|v|^{4}v
in
L^{p}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

\eqref{term2R}\to 0
in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-1}
.

\mathrm{(iv)}

\eqref{term3R}\to 0
in
L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
1\leq p<\infty
.

Consequently,
v
is a distributional solution of (
2.4
) on
(-T,T)
.

Proof.

(i) The estimate in
L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
is the same as the periodic case.
For the Sobolev norm, however, fractional Leibniz rule type estimates are needed to handle the exponential term which is not in
L^{p}
for any
p<\infty
.
We recall the following:

Lemma 2.7

(
[
11
, Lemma 2.7]
)
.

Let
f,f_{1},f_{2}
be real-valued functions in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then, for
0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2}
we have

\displaystyle\|e^{i\partial_{x}^{-1}f}g\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{2}}\big)\|g\|_{H^{s}},

\displaystyle\big\|\big(e^{i\partial_{x}^{-1}f_{1}}-e^{i\partial_{x}^{-1}f_{2}}\big)g\big\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\Big(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{L^{2}}+\big(1+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\big)\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{L^{1}}\Big)\|g\|_{H^{s}}.

From this lemma, we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\big)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle\|v_{N}-v\|_{L^{p}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}\big)\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty)

by Lemma
2.4
(i).

(ii) This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.3
, the Sobolev inequality and the convergence in (i).

(iii) We write
F_{N}:=P_{\leq N}(|u|^{2}u)-|u_{N}|^{2}u_{N}
.
We can estimate this term as

\displaystyle\|e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}\partial_{x}F_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\partial_{x}\big(e^{-i\lambda J(u_{N})}F_{N}\big)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{-1}}+\||u_{N}|^{2}F_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{1}}\lesssim\big(1+\|u_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}}}^{2}\big)\|F_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+\|u_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}}}^{2}\big)\Big(\big\|P_{>N}(|u|^{2}u)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{3}}}^{2}\|u_{N}-u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{3}}}\Big)\to 0\quad(N\to\infty),

where we have used several Sobolev embeddings, Lemma
2.4
(i) and the fact that
u\in C_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}-}
implies
|u|^{2}u\in C_{T}L^{2}
.

(iv) Since
v\in L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}
for any
p<\infty
, it suffices to show

\big\|\int_{-\infty}^{x}u_{N}\partial_{x}F_{N}\,dy\big\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}_{x}}\to 0\qquad(N\to\infty)

for any
p<\infty
.
Decomposing
u_{N}
and
F_{N}
into dyadic pieces and using an integration by parts, we estimate it as

\displaystyle\big\|\int_{-\infty}^{x}u_{N}\partial_{x}F_{N}\big\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\sum_{K_{1}\leq N}\sum_{K_{2}\leq K_{1}}\big\|\int_{-\infty}^{x}P_{K_{1}}u\cdot\partial_{x}P_{K_{2}}F_{N}\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big\|_{L^{p}_{T}}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{K_{1}\leq N}\|P_{K_{1}}u\cdot P_{K_{1}<\cdot\lesssim N}F_{N}\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{\infty}}+\Big\|\sum_{K_{1}\leq N}\sum_{K_{1}<K_{2}\lesssim N}\big\|\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{x}P_{K_{1}}u\cdot P_{K_{2}}F_{N}\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big\|_{L^{p}_{T}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\sum_{K_{1},K_{2}}\min\Big\{\frac{K_{1}}{K_{2}},\,\frac{K_{2}}{K_{1}}\Big\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{K_{1}}u\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|P_{K_{2}}F_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big\|_{L^{p}_{T}}+\sum_{K_{1}}\|P_{K_{1}}u\|_{L^{2p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|F_{N}\|_{L^{2p}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|F_{N}\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\sum_{K_{1}}\|P_{K_{1}}u\|_{L^{2p}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|F_{N}\|_{L^{2p}_{T}L^{\infty}}.

Since
\sum_{N}\|P_{N}u\|_{L^{2p}_{T}L^{\infty}}<\infty
by (
2.8
), we obtain the convergence from Lemma
2.4
(ii).
∎

This is the end of the proof of Proposition
2.2
.
∎

We have already exploited the
L^{4}L^{\infty}
refined Strichartz estimate with additional regularity (i.e., decay in (
2.8
)) for justification of the gauge transformed equations (
2.4
), (
2.6
).
In the normal form reduction argument, we will also need the
L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
control of the gauge transform.
This can be obtained either from the control of
u
in Proposition
2.1
and the definition of the gauge transformation (via fractional Leibniz rule) or by directly applying the short-time Strichartz argument to the equations (
2.4
), (
2.6
) we have just verified.
Here, we take the latter approach:

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
w\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}
be a distributional solution of (
2.4
) in the non-periodic case or (
2.6
) in the periodic case.
Then, for
T\in(0,1]
and
N\geq 1
, we have

\|P_{N}w\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\Big(1+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}+\|w\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

In particular, for
0\leq s<\frac{1}{4}
we have
w\in L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}
and

\|w\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}-s-}\Big(1+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}+\|w\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Moreover, for two such solutions
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, we have

\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}-s-}\Big(1+\|w_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}^{4}+\|w_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}^{4}\Big)\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}.

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
2.1
, the short-time Strichartz estimates imply

\displaystyle\|P_{N}w\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T}N^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+N^{-\frac{3}{4}}\|P_{N}F(w)\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+N^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\|F(w)\|_{L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}},

where

F(w):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &{-}\lambda w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}+i\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}|w|^{4}w&&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}),\\
&\begin{aligned} &{-}\lambda\Big(w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}-2P_{c}(w\partial_{x}\bar{w})w\Big)\\
&\quad+i\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|w|^{4})w-P_{c}(|w|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|w|^{2})w\Big)\end{aligned}&\qquad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}).\end{aligned}\right.

Applying Lemma
2.3
and the Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle\|F(w)\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}

\displaystyle\leq\|w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}+\|\text{other terms}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|w\|_{L^{2}}+\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{3}\Big)\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

This implies the first claimed estimate.
Given
M\geq 1
, summing up the estimate for
N\geq M
implies

\|P_{\geq M}w\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}\lesssim M^{s-\frac{1}{4}+}\Big(1+\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{4}+\|w\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

for
s<\frac{1}{4}
, while summing up for
N\leq M
and Hölder in
t
, Bernstein in
x
show

\|P_{\leq M}w\|_{L^{4}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}M^{s}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

for
s>0
and with a factor
\langle\log M\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}
for
s=0
.
Choosing
M\sim T^{-1}
, we obtain the second estimate.
Finally, we can show the difference estimate by using

\|P_{N}(w_{1}-w_{2})\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{T}N^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+N^{-\frac{1}{4}+}\|P_{N}(F(w_{1})-F(w_{2}))\|_{L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}}

and repeating the same argument.
∎

2.4
Coifman-Meyer type lemmas

All the results in this subsection hold equally for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
.

In the NFR argument, we analyze the interaction representation of a solution
w(t,x)
on the frequency space; i.e., consider the equation for
\omega(t,\xi):=\mathcal{F}_{x}[S(-t)w(t,\cdot)](\xi)
instead of
w
itself.
On the other hand, the (
L^{\infty}_{x}
-type) refined Strichartz estimates for
w(t,x)
are based on the Schrödinger dispersion and do not hold after taking the absolute value on the frequency side.
In the previous result for the Benjamin-Ono equation in
[
12
]
, the refined Strichartz estimates were used only to estimate
\partial_{t}\omega(t,\xi)
, and that part could be separated from the other (
L^{2}_{x}
-based) estimates, in which one can take absolute values of
\omega(t,\xi)
.
In our problem, we need to use the refined Strichartz estimates more extensively, and it seems difficult to separate these steps in a similar manner.
Consequently, we will need Coifman-Meyer type estimates of trilinear Fourier multiplier operators.
For instance, consider the
L^{2}_{x}
estimate of the following trilinear operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg[\psi_{N}(\xi)\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}}{\phi(\vec{\xi})}i\xi_{3}(\psi_{N_{1}}\omega_{1})(\xi_{1})(\psi_{N_{2}}\omega_{2})(\xi_{2})(\psi_{N_{3}}\omega_{3}^{*})(\xi_{3})\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=S(-t)P_{N}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{i\xi_{3}}{\phi(\vec{\xi})}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\bigg],

where

\omega^{*}(\xi):=\overline{\omega(-\xi)},\qquad\phi(\vec{\xi}):=\xi^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}+\xi_{3}^{2}=2\xi_{13}\xi_{23}.

Suppose that we want to estimate its
L^{2}_{x}
norm by

B\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{\infty}},

for instance, when the multiplier
b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}):=i\xi_{3}/(\xi_{13}\xi_{23})
obeys the bound

|b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})|\lesssim B,\qquad\text{whenever}\quad\psi_{N}(\xi_{123})\prod_{j=1}^{3}\psi_{N_{j}}(\xi_{j})\neq 0.

The classical Coifman-Meyer theorem requires a condition like

|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}b(\xi)|\lesssim_{\alpha}B|\xi|^{-|\alpha|},\qquad\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}),\quad\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}).

This condition seems too strong, however.
Moreover, the endpoint space
L^{\infty}
is often excluded in Coifman-Meyer type estimates in a general setting.

Here, thanks to the frequency localization, we can show the desired estimates under weaker assumptions, by the Fourier series expansion of
b
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
be dyadic integers, and assume that the multiplier
b:I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{2}}\times I_{N_{3}}\to\mathbb{C}
is
C^{2}
and satisfies

|\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{\xi_{3}}^{\alpha_{3}}b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})|\lesssim N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}}N_{2}^{-\alpha_{2}}N_{3}^{-\alpha_{3}}B\quad\text{on $I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{2}}\times I_{N_{3}}$}

for any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
with
|\alpha|\leq 2
.
Then, for any
p,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim B\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{p_{2}}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{p_{3}}}.

Proof.

Define a
C^{2}
function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by

f(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &b(N_{1}\eta_{1},N_{2}\eta_{2},N_{3}\eta_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{\psi}_{N_{j}}(N_{j}\eta_{j})&\quad&\text{on $[-\pi,\pi]^{3}$},\\
&\text{$2\pi$-periodic on $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.}&&\end{aligned}\right.

Let
\{\hat{f}(k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}
be the Fourier coefficients of
f
, so that

b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{\psi}_{N_{j}}(\xi_{j})=f\Big(\frac{\xi_{1}}{N_{1}},\frac{\xi_{2}}{N_{2}},\frac{\xi_{3}}{N_{3}}\Big)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}e^{i(\frac{\xi_{1}}{N_{1}}k_{1}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{N_{2}}k_{2}+\frac{\xi_{3}}{N_{3}}k_{3})}\hat{f}(k)

on
I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{2}}\times I_{N_{3}}
.
Using this representation, Minkowski, and Hölder, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}|\hat{f}(k)|\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}e^{i\frac{k_{1}}{N_{1}}\xi_{1}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})e^{i\frac{k_{2}}{N_{2}}\xi_{2}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad=c\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}|\hat{f}(k)|\Big\|(P_{N_{1}}w_{1})(\cdot+\frac{k_{1}}{N_{1}})(P_{N_{2}}w_{2})(\cdot+\frac{k_{2}}{N_{2}})(P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3})(\cdot+\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}})\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\|\hat{f}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})}\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{p_{2}}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{p_{3}}}.

Now, we observe that
b(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{\psi}_{N_{j}}(\xi_{j})
satisfies the same estimates as that assumed for
b
, and this implies
\|f\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim B
, hence
\|\hat{f}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim B
.
Substituting this bound to the above estimate, we obtain the claim.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
be dyadic integers, and assume that the multiplier
b:I_{N}\times I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{2}}\times I_{N_{3}}\to\mathbb{C}
is
C^{3}
and satisfies

|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{\xi_{3}}^{\alpha_{3}}b(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})|\lesssim N^{-\alpha_{0}}N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}}N_{2}^{-\alpha_{2}}N_{3}^{-\alpha_{3}}B\quad\text{on $I_{N}\times I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{2}}\times I_{N_{3}}$}

for any
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{4}
with
|\alpha|\leq 3
.
Then, for any
p,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Big\|P_{N}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}b(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim B\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{p_{2}}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{p_{3}}}.

Proof.

Proof is similar to the preceding lemma.
This time there are four variables, so we use
\|\hat{f}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{4})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{2+}(\mathbb{T}^{4})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{4})}
.
∎

The above two lemmas cannot be applied directly when there is a cancellation in
\xi_{13}
or
\xi_{23}
.
For instance, consider the case
N_{1}\gg N_{2}\sim N_{3}
.
We have
|\phi(\vec{\xi})|\sim N_{1}|\xi_{23}|
, but
|\xi_{23}|
may be much smaller than
N_{2}
, and the assumption on the multiplier
b=\xi_{3}/(\xi_{13}\xi_{23})
may not hold in this case (since
|\partial_{\xi_{2}}b|/|b|=|\xi_{23}|^{-1}\not\lesssim N_{2}^{-1}
).
To use in such a case, we prepare the following lemma:

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
be dyadic integers, and assume that
N_{1}\not\sim N_{2}\sim N_{3}
.
Then, for any
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{K\geq 1}\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
K\leq|\xi_{23}|<2K\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{\xi_{3}}{\xi_{13}\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\frac{N_{3}}{N_{1}\vee N_{3}}\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{p}}\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{q}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

This time we consider the Fourier series of the multiplier
b(\xi_{1},\xi_{3})=\frac{\xi_{3}}{\xi_{13}}\tilde{\psi}_{N_{1}}(\xi_{1})\tilde{\psi}_{N_{3}}(\xi_{3})
defined on
I_{N_{1}}\times I_{N_{3}}
to obtain

\frac{\xi_{3}}{\xi_{13}}\tilde{\psi}_{N_{1}}(\xi_{1})\tilde{\psi}_{N_{3}}(\xi_{3})=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{i(\frac{\xi_{1}}{N_{1}}k_{1}+\frac{\xi_{3}}{N_{3}}k_{3})}\hat{f}(k),

with

\|\hat{f}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\frac{N_{3}}{N_{1}\vee N_{3}}.

This expression gives

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
K\leq|\xi_{23}|<2K\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{\xi_{3}}{\xi_{13}\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}|\hat{f}(k)|\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{k_{1}}{N_{1}}\xi_{1}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{1}}w_{1}](\xi_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\times\frac{\mathbf{1}_{K\leq|\xi^{\prime}|<2K}}{\xi^{\prime}}\int_{\xi^{\prime}=\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{N_{1}}w_{1}\|_{L^{p}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}|\hat{f}(k)|\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}_{K\leq|\xi|<2K}}{\xi}\int_{\xi=\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}}.

By the Hausdorff-Young and the Hölder inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}_{K\leq|\xi|<2K}}{\xi}\int_{\xi=\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\Big\|\frac{\mathbf{1}_{K\leq|\xi|<2K}}{\xi}\int_{\xi=\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\Big\|\int_{\xi=\xi_{23}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{2}}w_{2}](\xi_{2})e^{i\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}}\xi_{3}}\mathcal{F}[P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3}](\xi_{3})\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\Big\|(P_{N_{2}}w_{2})(\cdot)(P_{N_{3}}\bar{w}_{3})(\cdot+\frac{k_{3}}{N_{3}})\Big\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|P_{N_{2}}w_{2}\|_{L^{q}}\|P_{N_{3}}w_{3}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

Therefore, the claim follows.
∎

3
Normal form reduction: Setup

From now on, we set
\lambda=-1
in (
1.1
) for simplicity.
Note that the sign of
\lambda
is not relevant in our argument.
Furthermore, there will be no essential differences between the proofs for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
.

In the previous section, we have seen that if
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}
is a distributional solution of (
1.1
), then its gauge transform
4
4

4

We will use the same letter
w
to denote the gauge transform of the solution
u
to (
1.1
) in both cases
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
.

w
belongs to
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
and it is a distributional solution of

\partial_{t}w-i\partial_{x}^{2}w=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}+\frac{i}{2}|w|^{4}w&&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}),\\
&w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}\!-\!2P_{c}(w\partial_{x}\bar{w})w\!+\!i\Big(\frac{1}{2}P_{\neq c}(|w|^{4})w\!-\!P_{c}(|w|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|w|^{2})w\Big)&\quad&(\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.1)

Recall that each term on the right-hand side of (
3.1
) (especially the first cubic term) is a well-defined distribution in
L^{4}_{T}H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.
Since the gauge transformation is invertible, the original problem of unconditional uniqueness for (
1.1
) is reduced to establishing uniqueness of solution
w
to (
3.1
) with a fixed initial condition in the class
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
.

3.1
Equation on the Fourier side

We estimate the solution mainly on the Fourier side, introducing a new unknown function
\omega(t,\xi)
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\omega(t,\xi):=\mathcal{F}[S(-t)w(t)](\xi)=e^{it\xi^{2}}\hat{w}(t,\xi);\\
w(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[e^{-it\xi^{2}}\omega(t,\xi)]=S(t)\check{\omega}(t).\end{gathered}

If
w\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap L^{4}_{T}B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
is a distributional solution of (
3.1
), then
\omega
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega(t,\xi)\ =\ \mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega(t)](\xi)+\mathcal{R}^{t}[\omega(t)](\xi),

(3.4)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega](\xi)

\displaystyle:=e^{it\xi^{2}}\mathcal{F}\big[\big(S(t)\check{\omega}\big)^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{S(t)\check{\omega}}\big](\xi)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|<1\end{smallmatrix}}e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}i\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{t}[\omega](\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|<1\end{smallmatrix}}e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}i\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})+e^{it\xi^{2}}\mathcal{F}\Big[\frac{i}{2}|S(t)\check{\omega}|^{4}S(t)\check{\omega}\Big](\xi)

in the non-periodic case and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega](\xi)

\displaystyle:=e^{it\xi^{2}}\mathcal{F}\big[\big(S(t)\check{\omega}\big)^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{S(t)\check{\omega}}\big](\xi)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
\xi_{13}\xi_{23}=0\end{smallmatrix}}i\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{t}[\omega](\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{i\xi}{2\pi}|\omega(\xi)|^{2}\omega(\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad+e^{it\xi^{2}}\mathcal{F}\Big[\frac{i}{2}P_{\neq c}(|S(t)\check{\omega}|^{4})S(t)\check{\omega}-iP_{c}(|S(t)\check{\omega}|^{2})P_{\neq c}(|S(t)\check{\omega}|^{2})S(t)\check{\omega}\Big](\xi)

in the periodic case,
5
5

5

We remove the contribution of the frequencies
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
satisfying
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|:=\min\{|\xi_{13}|,|\xi_{23}|\}<1
from the main cubic term.
This will be useful in applying the normal form reduction.
Note that the condition
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|<1
(or
\geq 1
) is equivalent to
\xi_{13}\xi_{23}=0
(or
\neq 0
) in the periodic case.

with the notation

\omega^{*}(\xi):=\overline{\omega(-\xi)},\qquad\phi(\vec{\xi}):=\xi^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}+\xi_{3}^{2}=2\xi_{13}\xi_{23}\quad\text{if $\xi=\xi_{123}$}.

Note that
\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega]
is (formally) written as

\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega](\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|\geq 1\end{smallmatrix}}e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}i\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3}).

Next, we consider the dyadic decomposition of the nonlinearity:

\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega(t)]=\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}\mathcal{N}^{t}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega(t)],

(3.5)

where

\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}^{t}[\omega](\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\psi_{N}(\xi)\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|\geq 1\end{smallmatrix}}e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}i\xi_{3}\big(\psi_{N_{1}}\omega\big)(\xi_{1})\big(\psi_{N_{2}}\omega\big)(\xi_{2})\big(\psi_{N_{3}}\omega\big)^{*}(\xi_{3}).

(3.6)

Hereafter, we use the notation

\omega_{N}:=\psi_{N}\omega,\quad\text{and similarly,}\quad w_{N}:=P_{N}w.

Also, we will basically disregard constants and signs in front of the nonlinear terms which are not essential in the proof.
Furthermore, we will omit the superscript
t
in
\mathcal{N}^{t}[\omega]
,
\mathcal{R}^{t}[\omega]
for notational convenience.
Hence, we simply write (
3.5
)–(
3.6
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}[\omega]

\displaystyle=\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}\psi_{N}(\xi)\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|\geq 1\end{smallmatrix}}e^{it\phi(\vec{\xi})}\xi_{3}\omega_{N_{1}}(\xi_{1})\omega_{N_{2}}(\xi_{2})\omega_{N_{3}}^{*}(\xi_{3}).

In the proof of Proposition
2.8
, we have estimated the cubic nonlinearity in
H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
using Lemma
2.3
.
In the next proposition, we refine the argument to obtain slightly stronger control in
H^{-\frac{1}{2}}+B^{0}_{1,\infty}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

We have

\|S(t)\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{N}[\omega]\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}+B^{0}_{1,\infty}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}

for any
w\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap B^{0+}_{\infty,1}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\omega:=\mathcal{F}S(-t)w
.
More precisely, we have the following estimates:

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{N}[\omega]-\Big(\sum_{N\sim N_{3}\gg N_{1},N_{2}}+\sum_{N_{1}\vee N_{2}\sim N_{3}\gg N\gg N_{1}\wedge N_{2}}\Big)\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega]\Big\|_{\hat{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}+}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big\|\sum_{N\sim N_{3}\gg N_{1},N_{2}}\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega]\Big\|_{\hat{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big\|S(t)\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big[\sum_{N_{1}\vee N_{2}\sim N_{3}\gg N\gg N_{1}\wedge N_{2}}\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega]\Big]\Big\|_{B^{0}_{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

Remark 3.2
.

(i) Note that

H^{-\frac{1}{2}+}\hookrightarrow H^{-\frac{1}{2}}\hookrightarrow H^{-\frac{1}{2}}+B^{0}_{1,\infty}\hookrightarrow B^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{2,\infty}\hookrightarrow H^{-\frac{1}{2}-}.

We use the space
B^{0}_{1,\infty}
only in the case
N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N\gg N_{2}
due to the lack of summability in
N
, as we see in the proof below.
If we could also treat this case in
H^{-\frac{1}{2}}
(an
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space), then the normal form argument would become substantially simpler.

(ii) A multilinear version of the estimate can be shown by the same argument.
In particular, for two functions
w_{1},w_{2}
(and
\omega_{m}:=\mathcal{F}S(-t)w_{m}
,
m=1,2
) we have the following difference estimate:

\displaystyle\big\|S(t)\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{N}[\omega_{1}]-\mathcal{N}[\omega_{2}]\big)\big\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}+B^{0}_{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(w_{1},w_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}+\|(w_{1},w_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|(w_{1},w_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We first note that the “resonant” portion
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|<1
excluded from
\mathcal{N}[\omega]
can be estimated even in
\hat{H}^{\frac{1}{2}-}
; in fact, for each
N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\psi_{N}(\xi)\int_{\begin{smallmatrix}\xi=\xi_{123}\\
|\xi_{13}|\wedge|\xi_{23}|<1\end{smallmatrix}}\big|\xi_{3}\omega_{N_{1}}(t,\xi_{1})\omega_{N_{2}}(t,\xi_{2})\omega_{N_{3}}^{*}(t,\xi_{3})\big|\Big\|_{\hat{H}^{\frac{1}{2}-}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{N\sim N_{2}}\Big\|\int_{\xi=\xi_{2}+\xi^{\prime}}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}-}\big|\omega_{N_{2}}(t,\xi_{2})\big|\cdot\mathbf{1}_{|\xi^{\prime}|<1}\int_{\xi^{\prime}=\xi_{13}}|\xi_{3}|\big|\omega_{N_{1}}(t,\xi_{1})\omega_{N_{3}}^{*}(t,\xi_{3})\big|\Big\|_{L^{2}}+\text{(symm.~term)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{N\sim N_{2}}N^{0-}\|w_{N_{2}}(t)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{N_{1}\sim N_{3}}\|w_{N_{1}}(t)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|w_{N_{3}}(t)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\text{(symm.~term)},

which is clearly summable in
N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
.
Therefore, we may estimate the whole interaction
w^{2}\partial_{x}\bar{w}
instead of
S(t)\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{N}[\omega]
.
We consider the estimate of localized terms

I(X):=\Big\|\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}P_{N}(w_{N_{1}}w_{N_{2}}\partial_{x}\bar{w}_{N_{3}})\Big\|_{X},

assuming
N_{1}\geq N_{2}
and dividing the analysis into the following four cases.

(i)
N_{2}\gtrsim N_{3}
. In this case, we have
N\lesssim N_{1}
.
By Hölder and Bernstein, we treat this case in
H^{-\frac{1}{2}+}
as

\displaystyle I(H^{-\frac{1}{2}+})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\\
N_{1}\geq N_{2}\gtrsim N_{3}\end{smallmatrix}}N_{1}^{0+}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}N_{3}\|w_{N_{3}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|w\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

(ii)
N_{1}\gg N_{3}\gg N_{2}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle I(H^{-\frac{1}{2}+})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{1}\gg N_{3}\gg N_{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}+}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}N_{3}\|w_{N_{3}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|w\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}.

(iii)
N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N_{2}
.
We further divide the case according to the size of
N
.
If
N\lesssim N_{2}
, we can estimate in
H^{-\frac{1}{2}+}
as

\displaystyle I(H^{-\frac{1}{2}+})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N_{2}}N_{2}^{0+}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}N_{3}\|w_{N_{3}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|w\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

If
N\sim N_{1}\sim N_{3}
, then

\displaystyle I(H^{-\frac{1}{2}+})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N_{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}+}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}N_{3}\|w_{N_{3}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|w\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}.

Finally, when
N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N\gg N_{2}
, we estimate in
B^{0}_{1,\infty}
to avoid summation in
N
as

\displaystyle I(B^{0}_{1,\infty})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N_{2}}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}N_{3}\|w_{N_{3}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|w\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(iv)
N_{3}\gg N_{1}
. We have
N\sim N_{3}
and

\displaystyle I(H^{-\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg[\sum_{N}\Big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{N_{1}\geq N_{2}}\|w_{N_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|w_{N_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}N\|w_{\sim N}\|_{L^{2}}\Big)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}\lesssim\|w\|_{B^{0+}_{\infty,1}}\|w\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

The proof is completed.
∎

3.2
Classification of frequency interactions

Starting from the equation (
3.4
), the term
\mathcal{R}[\omega]
is already controlled in
\hat{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
(see Lemma
4.1
below).
The problem is how to treat the cubic term
\mathcal{N}[\omega]
.
Since this part cannot be controlled in
\hat{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
due to the derivative loss, we need to apply the normal form reduction (NFR).
Basically, one application of NFR consists of a differentiation by parts
6
6

6

In practice, we work with the equations in the integral form.
Hence, we actually apply an integration by parts in
t
.

in
t
and application of the product rule,

\displaystyle\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}e^{it\phi}\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})=\partial_{t}\Big[\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi}}{i\phi}\xi_{3}\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})\Big]

\displaystyle-\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi}}{i\phi}\xi_{3}\Big\{(\partial_{t}\omega)(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})+\omega(\xi_{1})(\partial_{t}\omega)(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})+\omega(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})(\partial_{t}\omega)^{*}(\xi_{3})\Big\},

followed by substitution of the equation (
3.4
),

\displaystyle\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi}}{i\phi}\xi_{3}(\partial_{t}\omega)(\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})

\displaystyle=\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi}}{i\phi}\xi_{3}\mathcal{R}[\omega](\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})+\int_{\xi=\xi_{123}}\frac{e^{it\phi}}{i\phi}\xi_{3}\mathcal{N}[\omega](\xi_{1})\omega(\xi_{2})\omega^{*}(\xi_{3})\quad\text{(and so on)}.

We will apply this procedure to each localized cubic nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}_{N,N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}[\omega]
in a different manner, as outlined below.
Assume
N_{1}\geq N_{2}
by symmetry, and consider the following case separation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &N_{1}\sim N_{3}&\quad&\left\{\begin{aligned} &N_{1}\sim N_{2}&\quad&\left\{\begin{aligned} &N\sim N_{1}\\
&N_{1}\gg N\end{aligned}\right.\\
&N_{1}\gg N_{2}&\quad&\left\{\begin{aligned} &N\sim N_{1}\\
&N_{1}\gg N\gg N_{2}\\
&N\sim N_{2}\\
&N_{2}\gg N\end{aligned}\right.\end{aligned}\right.\\
&N_{1}\gg N_{3}&\quad&\left\{\begin{aligned} &N_{1}\sim N_{2}\\
&N_{1}\gg N_{2}\gg N_{3}\\
&N_{2}\sim N_{3}\\
&N_{3}\gg N_{2}\end{aligned}\right.\\
&N_{3}\gg N_{1}&&\end{aligned}\right.\quad\begin{aligned} \cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{2}\sim N_{3}\sim N\\
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{2}\sim N_{3}\gg N\\[2.0pt]
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{3}\sim N\gg N_{2}\\
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N\gg N_{2}\\
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N\sim N_{2}\\
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gg N_{2}\gg N\\[2.0pt]
\cdots\quad&N_{1}\sim N_{2}\gg N_{3}\\
\cdots\quad&(N\sim)\,N_{1}\gg N_{2}\gg N_{3}\\
\cdots\quad&(N\sim)\,N_{1}\gg N_{2}\sim N_{3}\\
\cdots\quad&(N\sim)\,N_{1}\gg N_{3}\gg N_{2}\\[2.0pt]
\cdots\quad&(N\sim)\,N_{3}\gg N_{1}\geq N_{2}\end{aligned}

We classify these 11 cases as in Table
1
.
